L1613: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1614: Age and gender did not appear to affect the incidence and severity of AEs reported by patients in the toripalimab monotherapy safety dataset. While there were differences in the incidence of AEs by region, some AEs were higher in the US and others in the ex-US patients. It is difficult to draw conclusions about the effect of region or race on AEs as there is no biologic plausibility to these differences based on the pharmacokinetic profile of toripalimab.
L1615: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1616: FDA reviewed the incidence of AEs by age and gender in JUPITER-02 and in the pooled safety dataset. Review of safety by race and ethnicity was not possible, given that all patients enrolled in JUPITER-02 and included in the pooled monotherapy dataset used for FDA analyses were Asian patients.
L1617: In JUPITER-02, 139 (95%) and 7 (5%) of patients treated with toripalimab were age &lt;65 and ≥65 years, respectively. The incidence of Grade 3 and 4 adverse events was generally similar between patients of age ≥65 and patients of age &lt;65 years. The exception included grade 3 and 4 platelet count decreased, which occurred in 29% vs. 8% in patients ≥65 and &lt;65 years, respectively. In the pooled safety dataset, 680 (80%) and 171 (20%) of patients were age &lt;65 and ≥65 years, respectively. The incidence of Grade 3 and 4 AEs was generally similar across the age groups, except for a higher incidence of alanine aminotransferase increased (2.3% vs. 0.6%), blood creatine phosphokinase increased (2.3% vs. 0.9%), and hyperglycemia (3.5% vs. 1.3%) in patients age ≥65 years vs. patients age &lt;65 years.
L1618: Among the 146 patients who received toripalimab in combination with cisplatin and gemcitabine in JUPITER-02, 22 (15%) were female and 124 (85%) were male. The incidence of Grade 3 and 4 AEs was similar in females compared with males, except for neutrophil count decreased and platelet count decreased, which occurred more frequently in female patients (32% vs. 14%; and 14% vs. 8%, respectively). In the pooled safety dataset, 298 (35%) patients were female and 553 (65%) were male. The incidence of Grade 3 and 4 AEs was similar in female versus male patients.
L1619: FDA agrees with applicant's position that age and gender did not appear to have a major impact on the safety profile of toripalimab in combination with cisplatin and gemcitabine or as a monotherapy.
L1620: ## 8.2.8. Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
L1621: ## Data:
L1622: This section contains information on specific safety issues such as organ impairment.
L1623: ## **Organ Impairment**
L1624: Like other monoclonal antibodies, toripalimab is metabolized to peptides and amino acids by target cell interaction and circulating phagocytic cells. Based on the metabolic pathway of monoclonal antibodies, formal organ impairment studies were not conducted and are not planned (Guidance for Industry, September 2020; Guidance for Industry, May 2003). Likewise, based on the metabolism of monoclonal antibodies, studies of drug-drug interaction and the ADME of toripalimab were not conducted.
L1625: ## **Drug-drug Interaction**
L1626: Monoclonal antibodies are expected to have limited drug-drug interactions. However, therapeutic proteins that alter cytokine levels may result in drug-drug interaction through regulation of the cytochrome P450 enzymes. Clinically meaningful cytokine release was not observed in nonclinical studies of toripalimab (Module 2.6.2 Section 2.6.2.8.2). While immunosuppressors may affect the clearance of some therapeutic proteins, corticosteroids and other immunosuppressants are not routinely administered with toripalimab but are limited to the treatment of irAEs (Guidance for Industry, August 2020). Based on these considerations, drug-drug interaction studies have not been conducted with toripalimab.
L1627: ## **Comorbid Diseases**
L1628: Patients with underlying autoimmune disease (except thyroid disease, diabetes adequately treated with replacement therapy, or vitiligo) have been excluded. This was due to concerns about toripalimab-induced exacerbation of the underlying autoimmune disease.
L1629: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1630: The evaluation of toripalimab in terms of organ impairment, drug-drug interactions, and co-morbid disease has been consistent with available guidance and with that of other monoclonal antibodies.
L1631: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1632: The information above is not relevant to this section of the review. No specific safety studies were included in the BLA.
L1633: ## 8.2.9. **Additional Safety Explorations**
L1634: ## Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
L1635: ## **Data:**
L1636: In accordance with ICH S6(R1) and ICH S9, carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted and none are planned.
L1637: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1638: The evaluation of toripalimab followed available FDA guidance and was consistent with that of other monoclonal antibodies.
L1639: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1640: FDA agrees that carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted with toripalimab and agrees with the Applicant's position.
L1641: ## Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
L1642: ## **Data:**
L1643: In accordance with ICH S6(R1), no reproductive and development toxicity studies have been conducted with toripalimab. There is extensive public information indicating that antibodies targeting PD-1 can cause fetal harm. This obviates the need for reproductive studies and the risk of fetal harm will be incorporated into product labeling.
L1644: Among the patients treated with toripalimab, one patient became pregnant and chose to terminate the pregnancy at Week 6.
L1645: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1646: The evaluation of toripalimab followed available FDA guidance and is consistent with that of other drugs in this class.
L1647: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1648: See FDA's Assessment in Section 5.5.4 (Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity) of this review.
L1649: ## Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
L1650: ## **Data:**
L1651: Toripalimab has not been studied in the pediatric population. An agreed iPSP proposing to seek a full waiver from the PREA requirements have been granted for patients with recurrent or metastatic NPC who have received prior platinum-based therapy. An agreed iPSP for patients who have
L1652: received no prior therapy for recurrent or primary metastatic disease has been submitted. Pediatric studies would be “impossible or highly impractical” given both the extreme rarity of the condition in the pediatric population and the large number of ongoing pediatric trials with PD-1/L1 inhibitors in a variety of pediatric tumor types. This BLA includes a request for waivers from PREA requirements.
L1653: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1654: Pediatric data is not available. PREA waivers have been requested in this BLA.
L1655: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1656: FDA agrees with Applicant's position.
L1657: ## **Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound**
L1658: ## **Data:**
L1659: The effects of overdose of toripalimab are unknown. No antidote is available and dialysis is not expected to affect the concentration of toripalimab. In animal toxicology studies, cynomolgus monkeys had no adverse effects with a single dose of 203 mg/kg or multiple doses of 100 mg/kg.
L1660: Toripalimab, as with other drugs of this class, is not expected to have a potential for abuse. The Applicant did not conduct studies to evaluate abuse potential. There is no evidence suggesting a risk for dependence and no withdrawal symptoms were observed during human clinical trials.
L1661: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1662: The evaluation of toripalimab considered available FDA guidance and was consistent with other drugs in this class.
L1663: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1664: FDA agrees with Applicant's position.
L1665: ## **8.2.10. Safety in the Postmarket Setting**
L1666: ## **Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience**
L1667: ## **Data:**
L1668: TopAlliance has synchronized their Periodic Safety Update Report (PSUR) with their Development Safety Update Report (DSUR). A PSUR/DSUR that covers the period from December 17, 2019 to December 16, 2020, was submitted to IND (b) (4) on February 8, 2021. Currently, toripalimab has received approval in China for:
L1669: - Treatment of patients with locally advanced or metastatic melanoma who have progressed on or after systemic therapy;
L1670: (b) (4)
L1671: (toripalimab-tpzi)
L1672: - Treatment of patients with recurrent or metastatic nasopharyngeal carcinoma who have progressed on or after second-line systemic therapy; and
- Treatment of patients with locally advanced/metastatic urothelial carcinoma who have progressed on or after platinum-containing chemotherapy or progressed within 12 months of neoadjuvant or adjuvant platinum-based therapy.
L1673: All indications are conditionally approved in China based on objective response rate in a single-arm clinical trial. The full approvals of these indications will depend on whether the long-term clinical benefit can be demonstrated in Chinese patients in the ongoing confirmatory clinical trials.
L1674: During this reporting period, the total sales of toripalimab injection (240 mg/vial) were [REDACTED] (b) (4) vials. The patient exposure during the reporting period was estimated to be [REDACTED] (b) (4) patient-years with an estimated total postmarketing exposure of [REDACTED] (b) (4) patient-years. Postmarketing safety reports were submitted for adverse drug reactions occurring in 195 patients during this reporting period. Adverse drug reactions reported in \ge 7 patients included myelosuppression, white blood cell count decreased, immune-mediated pneumonitis, rash, and liver function abnormal. Two patients experienced organ transplant rejection (liver) following toripalimab. In a clinical trial, 1 patient developed Grade 2 cytokine release syndrome after use of toripalimab in combination with a prostaglandin E2 receptor subtype 4 inhibitor.
L1675: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1676: The safety signals reported post-marketing in China were consistent with the adverse drug reactions reported with other PD-1 blocking antibodies (alone and in combination with chemotherapy). The risk of transplant rejection is currently included in the package insert of other PD-1 inhibitors and will be included in the proposed US toripalimab package insert. This risk will be added to the package insert in other regions.
L1677: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1678: FDA agrees with Applicant's position.
L1679: ## **Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting**
L1680: ## **Data:**
L1681: Base of the mechanism of action, the applicant expects that the immune-related AEs and infusion-related reactions will continue to be identified in the postmarketing setting.
L1682: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1683: TopAlliance will continue to monitor the postmarketing safety profile of toripalimab. The expected adverse events are predictable based on the mechanism of action of the drug class.
L1684: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1685: FDA agrees with Applicant's position.
L1686: ## **8.2.11. Integrated Assessment of Safety**
L1687: ## **Data:**
L1688: The safety of toripalimab in combination with platinum-based chemotherapy was evaluated in 158 patients with NPC and 48 patients with a variety of solid tumors. The safety of toripalimab monotherapy was evaluated in 190 patients with NPC and 876 patients with various solid tumors. The size of the safety dataset, the methods of data collection, and the data quality were adequate to characterize the safety profile of toripalimab.
L1689: A summary of the relevant safety information is included below.
L1690: - Among patients treated with toripalimab or placebo in combination with cisplatin/gemcitabine on JUPITER-02, there was 1 death in the toripalimab (epistaxis) and 2 deaths in the placebo arm (disease progression, cerebral infarction). In Cohort 3/POLARIS-02, there were 9 (4.7%) deaths in 190 patients with NPC who received toripalimab monotherapy. Causes of death included death NOS (N=3), disease progression (2), death/no cause recorded (1), hyperbilirubinemia (1), hyponatremia (1), and sudden death (1).
- Permanent discontinuation due to an AE (all grades) on JUPITER-02 occurred in 7.5% of patients in the toripalimab and 4.9% in the placebo arm. Causes of discontinuation (\ge 1\%) in the toripalimab arm were lung infection/pneumonia, rash, and pulmonary tuberculosis. Permanent discontinuation occurred in 8.9% of patients in Cohort 3/POLARIS-02. The most common AEs that led to drug discontinuation (\ge 1\%) were abnormal hepatic function and lung infection/pneumonia.
- Serious adverse events (all grades) occurred in 41.1% of patients on the toripalimab and 43.4% in the placebo arm of JUPITER-02. Events in \ge 2\% of patients in the toripalimab arm included lung infection/pneumonia, diarrhea, rash, and upper respiratory infection. In Cohort 3/POLARIS-02 24.2% of patients with NPC developed a SAE including (\ge 2\%) lung infection/pneumonia, hepatic function abnormal, and death NOS.
- The most common adverse events (\ge 10\%) and at a higher incidence (\ge 5\% all grades or \ge 2\% Grade 3-5) in the toripalimab arm than in the placebo arm of JUPITER-02 were hypothyroidism, pyrexia, diarrhea, rash, upper respiratory infection, pneumonia, and pruritus. The most common adverse events (\ge 10\%) in patients on Cohort 3/POLARIS-02 were hypothyroidism, asthenia, musculoskeletal pain, cough, pyrexia, decreased appetite, constipation, pruritus, and weight decreased.
L1691: (b) (4)
L1692: (toripalimab-tpzi)
L1693: - The most common laboratory abnormalities (\ge 20\%) or \ge 5\% (all grades) or \ge 2\% (Grade 3-5) in the toripalimab arm than in the placebo arm of JUPITER-O2, included anemia, leukopenia, lymphopenia, ALT increased, and LDH increased. In Cohort 3/POLARIS-O2 the most common laboratory abnormalities (&gt; 20\%) were lymphopenia, anemia, hypoalbuminemia, hyponatremia, increased AST, hypocalcemia, increased AKP, hypertriglyceridemia, hyperglycemia, and increased ALT.
- Immune-related AEs were reported in 26.7% of patients including 26.1% who received toripalimab monotherapy and 29.6% who received toripalimab in combination with platinum-based chemotherapy. These included hypothyroidism (15.3%), hyperthyroidism (5.7%), skin reactions (4.2%), hepatitis (2.7%), pneumonitis (2.2%), diabetes mellitus (0.9%), thyroiditis (0.6%), adrenal insufficiency (0.5%), pancreatitis (0.5%), platelet count decreased (0.5%), myocarditis (0.4%), myositis (0.4%), nephritis (0.4%), colitis (0.2%), hypophysitis (0.2%), iritis (0.1%), and uveitis (0.1%).
- Among all patients who received toripalimab (N = 1272), 33 (2.6%) patients developed an infusion-related reaction (IRR); there were no Grade 4-5 IRR and only 1 Grade 3 IRR. The incidence of IRR was 2.1% (22/1066) with toripalimab monotherapy and 5.3% (11/206) when toripalimab was given in combined with chemotherapy. In the toripalimab monotherapy safety dataset, IRR occurred in 1.0% of patients with ADA and in 2.2% of those without ADA. When toripalimab was combined with chemotherapy in JUPITER-O2 (N = 146), none of the 5 patients with ADA experienced an IRR as compared with a 4.3% incidence of IRR in patients who were ADA-negative.
L1694: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1695: Overall, toripalimab demonstrated a predictable and manageable safety profile that is expected, given its mechanism of action and consistent with other PD-1 blocking antibodies. The majority of adverse events were manageable with symptomatic management, corticosteroids, and/or drug interruption and were reversible upon drug discontinuation. The safety data submitted in the BLA support the use of toripalimab in patients with NPC with a favorable benefit-risk ratio in that the benefits of longer PFS (in the first-line) and durable responses which exceed that achieved with standard of care (in the first-line) or where no therapy exists (in the recurrent setting) outweigh the risks of these well-established and manageable toxicities.
L1696: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1697: FDA conducted the safety review of toripalimab in combination with cisplatin and gemcitabine in 146 patients with locally recurrent or metastatic NPC who received toripalimab 240 mg Q3W in combination with cisplatin and gemcitabine in the JUPITER-O2 trial. For toripalimab monotherapy, FDA conducted the safety review in 190 patients with locally recurrent or metastatic NPC whose disease progressed on or after platinum-containing chemotherapy and who received toripalimab 3
L1698: mg/kg Q2W as a single agent in Cohort 3 of POLARIS-02 and in a pooled dataset of 851 patients with various solid tumors who received toripalimab 3 mg/kg Q2W as a single agent.
L1699: FDA's review of deaths due to AE in JUPITER-02, Cohort 3 of POLARIS-02, and the pooled safety dataset did not raise concern for an unexpected safety signal relative to other agents in this class.
L1700: Discontinuation of toripalimab due to AEs occurred in 12% of patients in JUPITER-02, and in 9% and 11% of patients in the POLARIS-02/Cohort 3 trial and the safety pooled dataset, respectively. In JUPITER-02, the most frequent AEs leading to toripalimab discontinuation (\ge 1\%) were pneumonia, pulmonary tuberculosis, rash, vomiting, and thrombocytopenia. In Cohort 3 of POLARIS-02, the most frequent AEs leading to toripalimab discontinuation (\ge 1\%) were pneumonia, abnormal hepatic function, and hyperbilirubinemia.
L1701: Adverse events leading to toripalimab dose interruptions occurred in 50%, 23%, and 19% of patients in JUPITER-02, POLARIS-02/Cohort 3, and the pooled safety dataset, respectively. In JUPITER-02, the most frequent AEs leading to dose interruptions (\ge 2\%) were anemia, decreased neutrophil count, thrombocytopenia, pneumonia, acute kidney injury, fatigue, upper respiratory tract infection, and hypothyroidism. In POLARIS/Cohort 3, the most frequent AEs leading to dose interruptions (\ge 1\%) were pneumonia, thrombocytopenia, fatigue, hyperbilirubinemia, anemia, decreased appetite, abnormal hepatic function abnormal, hypothyroidism, and pneumonitis.
L1702: Serious adverse reactions events (SAEs) occurred in 43%, 24%, and 23% of patients in JUPITER-02, POLARIS/Cohort 3, and the pooled safety dataset, respectively. In JUPITER-02, the most frequent SAEs (\ge 2\%) were thrombocytopenia, decreased neutrophil count, pneumonia, anemia, abnormal hepatic function, and rash. In POLARIS/Cohort 3, the most frequent SAEs (\ge 2\%) were pneumonia, abnormal hepatic function abnormal, and hyperbilirubinemia.
L1703: Immune-related adverse events (irAEs) occurred in 40% (Grades 3-4, 6%) and 25% of patients (Grades 3-4, 6%) in JUPITER-02 and in the pooled safety dataset, respectively. In JUPITER-02, the most frequent irAEs (\ge 2\%) were hypothyroidism, dermatologic reactions, pneumonitis, and thyroiditis. In the pooled safety dataset, the most frequent irAEs (\ge 2\%) were hypothyroidism, hyperthyroidism, dermatologic reactions, hepatitis, and pneumonitis. Two patients in the pooled dataset had fatal immune-related pneumonitis. Overall, the observed incidence of irAEs was similar to that reported for other agents in this class, with the exception of a higher incidence of hypothyroidism, which is most likely related to the high proportion of patients who had received prior radiation as treatment for NPC.
L1704: Infusion-related reactions (IRRs) occurred in 4.1% and 2.0% of patients in JUPITER-O2 and the pooled safety dataset, respectively. All IRRs were Grades 1 or 2, except for one grade 3 IRR in the pooled safety dataset.
L1705: In JUPITER-O2, the most frequent AEs (\ge 20\%) were nausea, vomiting, decreased appetite, constipation, hypothyroidism, rash, pyrexia, diarrhea, peripheral neuropathy, cough, musculoskeletal pain, upper respiratory infection, insomnia, malaise, and dizziness. In POLARIS-O2/Cohort 3 the most frequent AEs (\ge 20\%) were hypothyroidism, fatigue, and cough, while in the pooled safety dataset evaluated by FDA (n=851), the most frequent AEs (\ge 20\%) were hypothyroidism, fatigue, and musculoskeletal pain.
L1706: In JUPITER-O2, the most frequent Grade 3 to 4 laboratory abnormalities (\ge 2\%) were decreased neutrophils, decreased lymphocytes, decreased hemoglobin, decreased platelets, decreased potassium, decreased sodium, increased alanine aminotransferase, increased magnesium, decreased magnesium, decreased calcium, increased aspartate aminotransferase, and increased bilirubin. In POLARIS/Cohort 3, the most frequent Grade 3 to 4 laboratory abnormalities (\ge 2\%) were decreased sodium, decreased hemoglobin, increased aspartate aminotransferase and increased alkaline phosphatase. In the pooled monotherapy safety dataset, the most frequent Grade 3 to 4 laboratory abnormalities (\ge 2\%) were decreased sodium, decreased lymphocytes, decreased hemoglobin, decreased fibrinogen, increased lipase, increased aspartate aminotransferase, increased glucose, decreased phosphate, increased alanine aminotransferase, and increased amylase.
L1707: The safety profile of toripalimab was similar to other PD1 blocking antibodies when combined with platinum-based chemotherapy or administered as a monotherapy. The most significant adverse reactions include immune-related adverse events, which are adequately addressed by the information in the Warnings and Precautions section included in product labeling. There were no significant safety concerns identified during BLA review requiring risk management beyond labeling or warranting consideration for Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS). Toripalimab, in combination with cisplatin or gemcitabine or as a monotherapy, appears to have an acceptable safety profile when assessed in the context of a life-threatening disease with unmet medical need.
L1708: ## 9 SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
L1709: ## 9.1. Statistical Issues
L1710: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1711: There were no major statistical issues identified during review of the application. For JUPITER-02, the hypothesis test for the primary endpoint of PFS per BIRC review was statistically significant, and the estimated PFS benefit is considered to be clinically meaningful. The secondary endpoint of ORR, which was tested after PFS null hypothesis was rejected, was also statistically significant and the magnitude of effect supported the finding of the primary endpoint of PFS. Finally, based on interim looks, the trend in survival benefit appeared to be in favor of the toripalimab arm. However, OS will be formally tested in the future, once 130 deaths are observed.
L1712: In POLARIS-02, there were no major statistical issues. The ORR in patients with NPC previously treated with platinum-based chemotherapy was clinically relevant, with durable responses observed. In general, the treatment benefit appeared to be similar across all relevant subgroups. The time-to-event endpoints of PFS and OS were considered to be descriptive in nature for this single arm study.
L1713: A minor statistical issue in this application included issues with documentation of histology subtypes for patients, as the Applicant's initial datasets did not adhere to WHO-recommended classifications. However, this was addressed during the course of the review cycle and the final histology descriptions were considered acceptable for the benefit:risk assessment by histology for this particular indication.
L1714: Also, both JUPITER-02 and POLARIS-02 were conducted in a single country, resulting in homogeneity of patient race and region. FDA analyses of marketing application data generally include subgroup analyses by key demographic characteristics such as age, sex, race, and region, to explore how consistent study findings are in this groups and if data may be applicable to the U.S. population. Given the population of this trial, FDA was not able to conduct subgroup analyses by race or region, resulting in the inability to examine consistency of trial results by these factors.
L1715: ## 9.2. Conclusions and Recommendations
L1716: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1717: (b) (4)
L1718: (toripalimab-tpzi)
L1719: The demonstration of a statistically significant and clinically meaningful improvement in PFS favoring the toripalimab plus chemotherapy arm in JUPITER-02, supported by a statistically significant improvement in ORR, along with a longer median DOR in the toripalimab plus chemotherapy arm vs the placebo plus chemotherapy arm and no evidence of a detrimental effect on OS, provides substantial evidence of effectiveness for toripalimab, in combination with cisplatin and gemcitabine, for the first-line treatment of adults with metastatic or with recurrent, locally advanced nasopharyngeal carcinoma (NPC). Regarding an indication for the treatment of adults with recurrent unresectable or metastatic NPC with disease progression on or after platinum-containing chemotherapy, the ORR of 21% with a median DOR of 14.9 months demonstrated in POLARIS-02 is considered clinically meaningful when considering the intended patient population, and the results of the JUPITER-02 study provide further confidence in the benefit of toripalimab for the treatment of advanced NPC.
L1720: The overall safety profile of toripalimab was similar to that observed with other PD-1 blocking antibodies. Safety issues identified as significant and serious during the BLA review were immune-related adverse events and infusion-related reactions, which are adequately addressed by the information in the Warnings and Precautions section included in product labeling. There were no significant safety concerns identified during BLA review requiring risk management beyond labeling or warranting consideration for Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS). Toripalimab appears to have an acceptable safety profile when assessed in the context of a life-threatening disease with unmet medical need.
L1721: (b) (4)
L1722: Section 21 CFR 314.106(b) outlines three requirements to establish the acceptability of foreign data as the sole basis for marketing approval:
L1723: 1. The foreign data are applicable to the U.S. population and U.S. medical practice.
L1724: - The studies have been performed by clinical investigators of recognized competence.
- FDA is able to validate the data through an onsite inspection or other appropriate means.
L1725: FDA will apply this policy in a flexible manner according to the nature of the drug and data being considered.
L1726: While both JUPITER-02 and POLARIS-02 were conducted exclusively in Asia, a flexible regulatory approach is warranted given the high unmet need and lack of available therapies for US patients with NPC. The comparator arm of cisplatin plus gemcitabine used in the JUPITER-02 study is considered standard of care for the first-line treatment of NPC in the US and is therefore applicable to US medical practice. The incidence of NPC is much higher in Southeast Asia relative to the United States, where NPC is a relatively rare disease, making enrollment to clinical trials more feasible in that region of the world. While a higher proportion of patients with NPC in the US have keratinizing histology compared to patients in China (25% vs 2%), the majority of patients with NPC even in the US have non-keratinizing histology. In addition, although clinical site inspections have not yet been completed due to restrictions related to the ongoing COVID-19 global pandemic, FDA plans to validate the data through onsite inspection. To further address the issue of applicability to the US population, to the Applicant should collect additional clinical data to further characterize the safety and efficacy of toripalimab in combination with cisplatin and gemcitabine for the treatment of NPC in a study population whose demographic and disease characteristics are more representative of the population of U.S. patients with NPC, which could be done as a postmarketing trial.
L1727: During review of this application, the Office of Pharmaceutical Quality (OPQ) identified a deficiency related to inadequate virus control in the manufacturing where the control strategy does not align with current industry standards and ICH guidelines. From an OPQ perspective, this BLA is not deemed approvable in its present form until the abovementioned deficiency is adequately resolved.
L1728: The clinical data supports a positive risk-benefit assessment and is supportive of regular approval toripalimab for the following indications:
L1729: - Toripalimab is indicated, in combination with cisplatin and gemcitabine, for the first-line treatment of adults with metastatic or with recurrent, locally advanced nasopharyngeal carcinoma (NPC).
L1730: - Toripalimab is indicated, as a single agent, for the treatment of adults with recurrent unresectable or metastatic NPC with disease progression on or after a platinum-containing chemotherapy who have not received prior therapy with an anti-PD-(L)1 antibody.
L1731: However, the overall risk-benefit assessment is unfavorable, based upon the deficiency identified during the review of the chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC). FDA therefore recommends a complete response for the application. The recommended indication for the post-platinum indication may change during subsequent review cycles as this was not yet negotiated with the Applicant.

L1772: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1773: The addition of toripalimab to gemcitabine and cisplatin has resulted in a clinically meaningful and statistically significant improvement in IRC-determined PFS and ORR with a doubling of the median duration of response. Single agent toripalimab has demonstrated a clinically meaningful response rate and a prolonged duration of response in a group of heavily pre-treated (median 4 prior therapies) patients with recurrent/metastatic NPC who have received prior platinum-based therapy. The safety profile is consistent with that of other PD-1/PD-L1 blocking antibodies.

L1776: ## 13 Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
L1777: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1778: The clinical review team determined that a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) was not required to ensure safe and effective use of toripalimab for the indicated populations. Recommendations for the safe and effective use of toripalimab are made in labeling and a patient package insert. There are no additional risk management strategies required beyond the recommended labeling. Although toripalimab can cause severe/serious toxicity, there are multiple other approved agents in the same class, and it will be prescribed by oncologists who, by training and experience, understand how to monitor and manage such toxicities.

L1913: ## 20.3. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology

L1916: ## 20.4. OCP Appendices (Technical documents supporting OCP recommendations)
L1917: ## 20.4.1. Population PK Analysis
L1918: ## 20.4.1.1 Executive Summary
L1919: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1920: (b) (4) (toripalimab-tpzi)
L1921: - Toripalimab PK could be described by a 2-compartment model with zero-order IV infusion and time-varying CL characterized by a sigmoidal-Emax function. The updated popPK analysis model contained 10430 PK observation records from 1014 patients with 25 different tumor types. Toripalimab CL decreases over time with a 50% maximal reduction after 65 days. There are no clinically relevant changes in clearance in different specific subgroups.
- Dosing in combination therapy: The proposed dose regimen of toripalimab 240 mg Q3W in combination with chemotherapy was the only tested dose in clinical trial and appears acceptable from clinical pharmacology perspective.
- Dosing in monotherapy: Sponsor proposed both 3mg/kg Q2W (b) (4) in (b) (4) toripalimab monotherapy treatment for previously treated NPC indication.
L1922: ## 20.4.1.2 PPK Assessment Summary
L1923: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1924: There are two PopPK models developed in support of BLA 761240. The first model utilized a dataset that was limited to patients who received toripalimab as a single agent (i.e., monotherapy). This popPK analysis model was conducted on a database containing 9377 PK observation records in 862 patients with various cancers. The second (updated) model was developed to assess the effects of concurrent chemotherapy on toripalimab PK and utilized a combined dataset that included all patients in the original popPK model and additional data from two studies, POLARIS-02/CT5 cohorts 5-8 and JUPITER-02, in which patients received toripalimab in combination with one of several platinum-based chemotherapy regimens. The updated popPK analysis model contained 10430 PK observation records from 1014 patients with various cancers.
L1925: In both the initial and updated popPK models, the structural PK model for toripalimab is a 2-compartment model with zero-order intravenous infusion and time-varying clearance (CL) characterized by a sigmoidal-maximum effect function as shown in Figure 24. There were no substantive differences between the two models; thus, summary statements will generally be based on the results of the updated model that includes from patients who received toripalimab administered as monotherapy or in combination with platinum-based chemotherapy. The
L1926: following summary from the updated popPK model is presented below (except where noted, all information presented below is from the updated popPK model).
L1927: APPEARS THIS WAY ON ORIGINAL
L1928: **Figure 24 Model Structure for Updated popPK Model**
L1929: Source: page 59 of TOPA-PMX-TORI-2412-COMB-THER-Amendment 1
L1930: Note: Abbreviations: CL=clearance; Emax=maximum change in clearance; PK=pharmacokinetic; Q=intercompartmental clearance; Time=time since first dose; T_{50}=time a t which the change in clearance is a t 50% of E_{max}; V1=volume of distribution of the central compartment; V2=volume of distribution of the peripheral compartment; \gamma=sigmoidicity factor.
L1931: The updated popPK model of toripalimab provides a robust characterization of PK in more than 1000 patients with cancer, that includes 25 different tumor types. The key results of updated popPK analyses are summarized as follows:
L1932: - Toripalimab CLDecreases over time with a 50% maximal reduction after 65 days
- Increases with increasing body weight, lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), creatinine clearance derived using the Cockcroft-Gault Equation (CRCL), and antidrug antibody-positive status
- Increases with decreasing albumin (ALB)
- Is higher in males than in females after adjustment for weight
- None of these effects resulted in clinically relevant changes in CL.
- Toripalimab volume of distribution of the central compartmentIncreases with increasing body weight
- Is lower in White race compared to the Asian race
- Neither of these effects resulted in clinically relevant changes in volume
L1933: Both models are fit-for-purpose and accurately predict the observed data; thus, the modeling and simulation results are considered reliable.
L1934: (b) (4)
L1935: PopPK modeling, which included 142 patients with mild and 1 patient with moderate hepatic impairment and 447 patients with mild renal impairment, is consistent with the expectation that renal and hepatic impairment do not affect the pharmacokinetics of toripalimab. Based on the expected metabolism of toripalimab and PK modeling, no dose modifications are needed in patients with mild or moderate hepatic or mild renal impairment. Although sex, body weight and race were statistically significant covariates in the popPK model, none of these covariates are considered clinically relevant in the context of the relatively flat E-R relationships of efficacy and safety; therefore, no dose modifications are needed based on race, sex, or body weight. Finally, since concurrent administration of platinum-based chemotherapy or underlying tumor type were not significant covariates, no dose modifications are needed for these factors.

L1975: ## 20.4.2. Exposure-Response Analysis
L1976: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1977: Exposure-efficacy analyses utilized datasets limited to patients enrolled in the pivotal studies (POLARIS-02; CT5/cohort 3 and JUPITER-02) in support of the second or greater-line and first-line indications, respectively. The exposure-safety analyses utilize the pooled toripalimab
L1978: monotherapy safety dataset for the second-line indication and safety datasets, in which data were limited to patients received toripalimab in combination with platinum-based chemotherapy enrolled in POLARIS-02/CT5 cohorts 5-8 and JUPITER-02. Model evaluation followed the same general principles in the pop PK analysis.
L1979: The E-R analyses for efficacy were not consistent for toripalimab monotherapy and for toripalimab administered in combination with chemotherapy, in that the former suggested a correlation between the exposure and efficacy whereas the latter showed no relationship over the exposure range. The findings from the E-R analyses of JUPITER-02 (CT15) are considered more reliable for the following reasons: 1) The same findings (no E-R relationship for efficacy) were demonstrated across two different endpoints (best overall response and PFS), 2) the efficacy dataset was larger in JUPITER-02 as compared to POLARIS-02(CT5)/cohort 3, and 3) the exposures in the lowest quartile were largely within the target trough concentration for toripalimab of \ge 20 \mu\text{g/mL}, which is approximately 3-fold higher than the concentration needed to ensure full receptor occupancy in peripheral blood mononuclear cells.
L1980: In contrast, the E-R analyses for safety were similar among patients receiving toripalimab as monotherapy and those receiving toripalimab in combination with chemotherapy, in that both analyses suggested decreased risks of clinically significant toxicities (Grade \ge 3 AEs, treatment-related Grade \ge 3 AEs, or AEs resulting in drug discontinuation) with increasing doses. While consistent, as noted above, this finding is not biologically plausible and it is likely that these findings are confounded by the complex pharmacokinetic profile of toripalimab with time-varying clearance and drug accumulation.
L1981: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1982: Among patients who received toripalimab monotherapy or in combination with chemotherapy, a flat exposure response relationship was identified for ORR. The flat ER relationship should be interpreted with caution as it was derived based on a small sample size and narrow exposure range from a single dose level.
L1983: There also appears to be no apparent relationship between toripalimab exposure and Grade \ge 3 AEs. The observed trend of reversed ER relationship for safety is inconclusive and could be due to other unknown confounding effects.
L1984: ## 20.4.2.1 ER (efficacy) Assessment Summary
L1985: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1986: Exposure-response relationships with efficacy for toripalimab monotherapy and combination therapy were evaluated in two separate analyses. A summary of the studies included, sample
L1987: sizes, dose levels, efficacy endpoints and exposure metrics evaluated in each analysis is provided in Table 60.

L1990: 1 Derived using posterior Bayesian estimates from the final population PK model. For efficacy, the average concentration was derived from the last dose to achieve the “highest” exposure for all PK subjects in both studies. Actual dosing histories were used in the final pop PK model to derive individual PK parameters and exposures.
L1991: Logistic regression analyses were conducted to describe the relationship between toripalimab exposure and the probability of achieving an objective response (complete or partial response per RECIST v1.1) in patients with NPC who received toripalimab as monotherapy and in patients with NPC who received toripalimab in combination with gemcitabine and cisplatin chemotherapy for up to 6 cycles. The average toripalimab concentration was included as the exposure predictor in the efficacy E-R model as it is the most representative exposure metric of average exposure over a dosing interval.
L1992: E-R relationships for PFS were also evaluated for patients enrolled in JUPITER-02, in which toripalimab was administered in combination with gemcitabine and cisplatin chemotherapy. An E-R analysis was conducted to evaluate potential predictors of PFS, univariate Kaplan-Meier (KM) survival curves were prepared for all available predictors, and the p-values from the log-rank test were calculated. In order to separate the impact of exposure from the other predictors, KM plots were generated for control and treatment groups. To include a predictor in
L1993: the Cox proportional hazard modeling, a predictor must be significant for both the control and treatment KM plots (p &lt; 0.001).
L1994: Predictors were evaluated in full for the full set of (nonredundant) explanatory variables. The full set of predictors are provided in Table 61 below. The initial model considered only the main effect parameters for each covariate (i.e., no interaction terms). Final models were obtained using a stepwise backward elimination. Model evaluation followed similar general principles in the pop PK analysis. For logistic regression, model discriminatory performance was assessed by VPCs.

L1997: ## Results:
L1998: Different relationships between drug exposures and efficacies were observed when toripalimab as administered as monotherapy compared with toripalimab administered with gemcitabine and cisplatin. The ER analysis for toripalimab suggested a correlation between toripalimab exposure and efficacy, whereas a flat exposure-response relationship was observed for efficacy in the ER analysis for toripalimab administered with gemcitabine and cisplatin.

L2001: summarized as follows: There were 36 responders among 161 patients in whom PK data were collected.

L2008: The key results of the exposure-response (E-R) analyses are summarized as follows:
L2009: - Toripalimab C_{ave} was the strongest predictor of being a responder.A subject with a median model predicted C_{ave} (39 µg/mL) has a 21% chance of being a responder.
- Subjects at the C_{ave} 5th percentile (p5) were 3 times more likely to be a responder than a reference subject, which is stronger than the Emax of all other predictors in the final model.
- The probability of being a responder for a hypothetical reference patient who received no toripalimab is 3.1%.
- Body weight: Subjects with a lower body weight have a greater probability of being a responder.Subjects at the p5 baseline weight (43.2 kg) were 2.3 times as likely to be a responder as a subject at the median baseline weight (60.2 kg).
- Baseline ALT: Subjects with a higher baseline ALT are more likely to be a responder.Subjects at the 95th percentile baseline ALT (51.1 U/L) were 2.7 times as likely to be a responder as a subject at the median baseline ALT (16.9 U/L).
- BALB: Subjects with a lower BALB are more likely to be a responder.
L2010: - Subjects at the p5 BALB (34.9 g/L) were 2.9 times as likely to be a responder as a subject at the median BALB (43.4 g/L).
- Baseline BILI: Subjects with a lower baseline BILI are more likely to be a responder.Subjects at the p5 BALB (0.26 mg/dL) were 1.5 times as likely to be a responder as a subject at the median baseline BILI (0.51 mg/dL).
- Baseline LDH: Subjects with a lower baseline LDH are more likely to be a responder.Subjects at the p5 BALB (134 U/L) were 1.1 times as likely to be a responder as a subject at the median baseline LDH (0.51 U/L).
- Renal impairment: Subjects with renal impairment are less likely to be a responder (0.25-fold of a reference subject).

L2073: ## **20.4.2.2 ER (safety) Assessment Summary**

L2075: The E-R relationships for safety were evaluated separately in patients with various tumor types who received toripalimab as monotherapy and in those who received toripalimab in combination with platinum-based chemotherapy. L2076: TAB001-01). The dataset for evaluation of the safety E-R relationship for toripalimab administered with platinum-based chemotherapy included 152 patients who had both PK and adverse event (AE) information enrolled in Study CT5 (POLARIS-02) cohorts 5-8 or Study CT15(JUPITER-02). A summary of the studies included, sample sizes, dose levels, efficacy endpoints and exposure metrics evaluated in each analysis is provided in Table 65.

L2164: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2165: FDA agrees that there appears to be no apparent relationship between toripalimab exposure and Grade \ge3 AEs. The observed trend of reversed ER relationship for safety could be due to other unknown confounding effects.
L2166: ## **Overall E-R conclusions:**
L2167: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L2168: As described above, the ER efficacy analysis for toripalimab monotherapy showed that the probability of being a responder increases with increasing C_{ave} (Figure 30). However, the ER efficacy analysis for toripalimab when administered with gemcitabine and cisplatin chemotherapy for up to 6 cycles showed a flat E-R relationship for observed objective response rates within quartiles among patients who received any amount of toripalimab (Figure 33). Patients receiving 240 mg toripalimab in combination with chemotherapy were 3.58 times as likely to be a responder as a patient receiving chemotherapy only (Figure 34). Additionally, the toripalimab C_{ave} levels did not influence PFS across patients from different exposure quantiles (Figure 37 and Figure 38). This observation indicates the proposed dosage has been optimized and aligned with the flat relationship of E-R analysis for objective response rates in JUPITER-02.
L2169: (b) (4) (toripalimab-tpzi)
L2170: The findings from the E-R analyses of JUPITER-02 (CT15) among patients receiving any toripalimab exposure are considered more reliable for the following reasons: 1) The same ER relationships were demonstrated across two different endpoints (best overall response and PFS) in JUPITER-02, 2) the efficacy dataset was larger in JUPITER-02 as compared to POLARIS-02(CT5)/cohort 3, and the exposures in the lowest quartile were largely within the target trough target concentration for toripalimab of 20 µg/mL, which is approximately 3-fold higher than the concentration needed to ensure full receptor occupancy in peripheral blood mononuclear cells, thus it is biologically plausible that there is no relationship between exposure and efficacy over the exposures achieved with toripalimab 3 mg/kg Q2W and toripalimab 240 mg Q3W.
L2171: The E-R relationships for safety were similar among patients receiving toripalimab as monotherapy and those receiving toripalimab in combination with platinum-based chemotherapy, in that both analyses suggested decreased risks of clinically significant toxicities (Grade ≥3 AEs, treatment-related Grade ≥3 AEs, or AEs resulting in study drug discontinuation) with increasing doses. Exposure to toripalimab does not appear to be associated with any of the 3 AEs evaluated. While consistent, this finding is not biologically plausible and it is likely that these findings are confounded by the complex pharmacokinetic profile of toripalimab with time-varying clearance and drug accumulation. Another possibility would be that patients who experienced AEs or SAEs are more likely to drop out early.
L2172: The clinical pharmacology profile of toripalimab is well established, based on robust PK characterization in more than 1000 patients across 25 tumor types and extensive understanding of E-R relationships for safety and efficacy of toripalimab across cancer indications. Analyses of safety of toripalimab primarily based on 862 patients who received toripalimab as monotherapy in 12 clinical studies and 152 patients with nasopharyngeal cancer patients (CT15) in a randomized clinical study showed flat dose-response and exposure-response relationships, with similar safety outcomes over the fixed 240 mg Q3W and body weighted dose 3 mg/kg Q2W. ER efficacy analysis for combinatorial therapy showed no relationship over the exposure range. These analyses support that toripalimab 240 mg Q3W and 3 mg/kg Q2W doses provide near maximal efficacy and an acceptable safety profile, with no advantage to dosing higher or more frequently given the fact that toripalimab has a wide therapeutic index.
L2173: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2174: Among patients who received toripalimab monotherapy or in combination with chemotherapy, a flat exposure response relationship was identified for ORR. The flat ER relationship should be interpreted with caution as it was derived based on a small sample size and narrow exposure range from a single dose level.
L2175: There also appears to be no apparent relationship between toripalimab exposure and Grade ≥3 AEs. The observed trend of reversed ER relationship for safety could be due to other unknown
L2176: confounding effects.
L2177: ## 20.5. Additional Safety Analyses Conducted by FDA
L2178: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2179: Not applicable. All safety analyses conducted by FDA are captured in the body of the review.

L2218: Figure 4 consists of two graphs, A and B, showing the effect of toripalimab (TAB001) on tetanus toxoid T cell response. Graph A is a line graph showing CFSE dilution (%) versus mAb concentration (µg/ml) on a logarithmic scale (0.01 to 10). TAB001 (red line) shows increasing CFSE dilution up to 0.3 µg/ml (peak around 50%), then decreasing. Control Ig (green line) remains low (around 10%). Graph B is a bar graph showing IFNγ concentration (pg/ml) versus mAb concentration (µg/ml) on a logarithmic scale (10.00 to 0.01). TAB001 (red bars) shows high IFNγ levels (around 3000 pg/ml) at 10.00 and 3.00 µg/ml, decreasing significantly at lower concentrations. Control Ig (black bars) shows negligible IFNγ levels.

L2223: Line graph showing Tumor volume (mm3) over time (D6 to D23) for various treatment groups. The Y-axis ranges from 0.0 to 600.0 mm3. The X-axis shows days D6, D7, D9, D12, D14, D16, D20, D21, D23. The legend indicates: Saline (open circles), KLH (black squares), TAB001 (0.3 mg/kg) (green triangles), TAB001 (1 mg/kg) (blue diamonds), TAB001 (3 mg/kg) (yellow stars), and TAB001 (10 mg/kg) (red inverted triangles). Tumor volume increases significantly for all groups over time. Asterisks (*) denote statistical significance.

L2230: Figure 16 is a density plot showing the distribution of Average Concentration (\mu\text{g/mL}) for Responders and Non-Responders. The x-axis ranges from 0 to 150 \mu\text{g/mL}, and the y-axis represents density, ranging from 0.000 to 0.025. The distribution for Non-Responders (red area) peaks around 30 \mu\text{g/mL} and extends up to approximately 100 \mu\text{g/mL}. The distribution for Responders (blue area) peaks around 70 \mu\text{g/mL} and extends up to approximately 150 \mu\text{g/mL}. The distributions show significant overlap, particularly between 50 and 100 \mu\text{g/mL}, indicating that responders and non-responders can have similar average concentrations.
L2231: Figure 17: A scatter plot showing the predicted probability of an Adverse Event (AE) versus CAVE (average concentration) in micrograms per milliliter. Data points are shown as green circles (Adverse Event) and blue circles (No Adverse Event). A solid red line represents the predicted probability, and a gray shaded area represents the 95% Confidence Interval (CI). The predicted probability of an AE is high (near 1.00) at low CAVE concentrations and decreases sharply as CAVE increases, approaching 0.00 at CAVE = 600.

L2238: Diagram illustrating the Model Structure for the Updated popPK Model. The model consists of two compartments: Central Compartment (V1) and Peripheral Compartment (V2).
A Zero order infusion rate feeds into the Central Compartment (V1).
Intercompartmental clearance (Q) transfers drug from the Central Compartment (V1) to the Peripheral Compartment (V2).
Clearance (CL) removes drug from the Central Compartment (V1).
The clearance (CL) is time-varying, defined by the equation: CL = CL \cdot e^{\left(\frac{E_{max} \cdot Time^\gamma}{T_{50} + Time^\gamma}\right)}

L2251: Figure 39 is a scatter plot showing the Clinical Event (Y-axis, ranging from 0.00 to 1.00) versus CAVE (ng/mL) (X-axis, ranging from 0 to 600). The plot displays observed data points and a model prediction.

Model Prediction: A solid red line represents the predicted probability of experiencing a toxicity Grade \ge 3. A gray shaded area surrounds the red line, representing the 95% Confidence Interval (CI) of the model prediction.
The observed data shows that for low CAVE concentrations (near 0 ng/mL), the probability of an Adverse Event is high (around 0.45), and the probability of No Adverse Event is low (around 0.55). As CAVE concentration increases, the predicted probability of an Adverse Event decreases, approaching 0.00 at high CAVE concentrations (around 500 ng/mL).
